Table 1.
Name conventions for variables

A
global language
specifies the expected interactions of a
system while a
local language
specifies the communication
behaviour of a participant.

Definition

(Global language)
.

A
global language
(
g-language
for short) is a
prefix-closed language
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
on
\Sigma_{\text{int}}
such that

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{ptp}}({\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}})
is finite.

Example
.

For any finite subset
\Sigma
of
\Sigma_{\text{int}}
, the set
\Sigma^{\star}

is a g-language; notice that, if
\mathfrak{P}
is infinite, then

\Sigma_{\text{int}}^{\star}
is not a g-language since it encompasses infinitely
many participants.

The set

\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{pref}}({\{\,\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{w}}}$}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{g}}}$},\,\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{w}}}$}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{g}}}$},\,\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{w}}}$}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{w}}}$}\,\}})
is a g-language (which will be used later on
in other examples).
It formally describes the following interaction protocol involving

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}
lice,
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}
ob and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}
arol: Carol can decide to
ask either Bob or Alice to
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{w}}
ork and after that Alice

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{g}}
ossips with Bob, unless also Bob is asked to work after
Alice is.

\diamond

Definition

(Local language)
.

A
local language
(
l-language
for short) is a
prefix-closed language
\mathbb{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
on
\Sigma_{\text{act}}
such that

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{ptp}}({\mathbb{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}})
is finite.
An l-language is
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}
-local
if its words have all actions
with subject
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}
.

Example
.

The set

\mathbb{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}=\{\,\varepsilon,\,\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{!}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{w}}}{}$},\,\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{!}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{w}}}{}$},\,\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{!}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{w}}}{}$}\cat\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{!}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{w}}}{}$}\,\}
is
a
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}
-local language; in particular, it specifies the local
behaviour of
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}
arol with respect to the g-language
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
of

Section

2
.

\diamond

As discussed in the Introduction,
l-languages give rise to
communicating systems
.

Definition

(Communicating system)
.

Let
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}\subseteq\mathfrak{P}
be a finite set of
participants.
A
(communicating) system over
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}
is a map

S=(\mathbb{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}})_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}}
assigning, to each participant

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}
, an
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}
-local
language
\mathbb{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}\neq\{\,\varepsilon\,\}
such that

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{ptp}}({\mathbb{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}})\subseteq\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}
.

By projecting a g-language
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
on a participant
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}
we obtain
the
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}
-local language describing the sequence of actions performed
by
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}
in the interactions involving
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}
in the words of
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
.

Section

2
below recasts in our setting the notion of
projection used in several choreographic formalisms, e.g., in
[
CHY12
,
HYC16
]
.

Definition

(Projection)
.

The
projection on
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}
of an interaction

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}}

is
computed by the function

\_{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\_}}}}:\Sigma_{\text{int}}\times\mathfrak{P}\to\Sigma_{\text{act}}\cup\{\,\varepsilon\,\}
defined by:

(\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}$}){\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}=\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{!}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}{}$}\qquad\qquad(\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}$}){\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}=\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}{}$}\qquad\qquad(\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}$}){\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}}}=\varepsilon

and extended homomorphically to interaction words and g-languages.
The
projection of a g-language
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
, written

\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}
, is the communicating system

{(\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}}})_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{ptp}}({\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}})}}
.

Example
.

Let
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
be the g-language in
Section

2
.
By
Section

2
, we have

\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}=(\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}})_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}\in\{\,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}},{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}\,\}}
where

\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}=\{\,\varepsilon,\,\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{w}}}{}$}
,

\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{w}}}{}$}\cat\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{!}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{g}}}{}$},\,\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{!}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{g}}}{}$}\,\}
,

\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}=\{\,\varepsilon,\,\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{g}}}{}$},\,\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{w}}}{}$}
,
\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{w}}}{}$}\cat\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{g}}}{}$}\,\}
, and

\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}}}=\{\,\varepsilon,\,\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{!}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{w}}}{}$},\,\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{!}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{w}}}{}$},\,\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{!}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{w}}}{}$}\cat\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{!}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{w}}}{}$}\,\}
.
The latter is the l-language in
Section

2
.

\diamond

We consider a
synchronous
semantics of communicating systems,
similarly to other choreographic approaches such
as
[
BZ07
,
CHY12
,
DGJ+15
,
SD19
]
.
Intuitively, a choreographic word is in the semantics of a system

S
iff its projection on each participant
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}
yields a word in
the local language assigned by
S
to
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}
.

Definition

(Semantics)
.

Given a system
S
over
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}
, the set

{\llbracket S\rrbracket}=\{\,w\in\Sigma_{\text{int}}^{\infty}\;\big|\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{ptp}}({w})\subseteq\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}\text{ and for all }\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}},\ w{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}}}\in S(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}})\,\}

is the
(synchronous) semantics
of
S
.

Notice that the above definition coincides with the
join

operation in
[
FBS04
]
, used in realisability conditions for an
asynchronous setting.

By the finiteness condition on the number of participants in a
g-language (
Section

2
) we immediately get the following.

Fact
.

Let
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
be a g-language. Then
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}\subseteq\Sigma_{\text{int}}^{\star}

implies
{\llbracket\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}\rrbracket}\subseteq\Sigma_{\text{int}}^{\star}
.

Example
.

For the system
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}
in
Section

2
, we have

\displaystyle{\llbracket\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}\rrbracket}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{pref}}({\{\,\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{w}}}$}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{g}}}$},\ \scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{w}}}$}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{g}}}$},\ \scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{g}}}$},\ \scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{w}}}$}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{w}}}$}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{g}}}$}\,\}})

\diamond

Two interactions
\alpha
and
\beta
are
independent
(in
symbols
{}\parallel{}
) when

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{ptp}}({\alpha})\cap{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{ptp}}({\beta})=\emptyset
.
Informally, a language
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
describing the behaviour of a system,
and containing a word
w
, does contain also all the words where
independent interactions in
w
are swapped; we say that

\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
is
concurrency closed
.
The notion of concurrency closure in our setting is a delicate one
because of the possible presence of infinite words.
One in fact has to allow infinitely many swaps of independent interactions while avoiding that
the interactions do disappear by pushing them infinitely far away.
Technically, we consider Mazurkiewicz’s traces
[
Maz86
]
on

\Sigma_{\text{int}}
with independence relation
{}\parallel{}
:

Definition

(Concurrency closure)
.

Let
\sim
be the reflexive and transitive closure of the relation

\equiv
on finite interaction words defined by

w\,\alpha\,\beta\,w^{\prime}\equiv w\,\beta\,\alpha\,w^{\prime}
where
{}\parallel{}
.
Following
[
Gas90
, Def. 2.1]
,
\sim
extends to

\Sigma_{\text{int}}^{\omega}
by defining

\text{for all }w,w^{\prime}\in\Sigma_{\text{int}}^{\omega}:\qquad w\sim w^{\prime}\quad\text{ iff }\quad w\ll w^{\prime}\quad\text{and}\quad w^{\prime}\ll w

where

w\ll w^{\prime}
iff for each finite prefix
w_{1}
of

w
there are a finite prefix
w_{1}^{\prime}
of
w^{\prime}
and a
g-word
\hat{w}\in\Sigma_{\text{int}}^{\star}
such that

w_{1}\cat\hat{w}\sim w_{1}^{\prime}
.
A g-language
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
is
concurrency closed
if it coincides
with its concurrency closure, namely

\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}=\{\,w\in\Sigma_{\text{int}}^{\infty}\;\big|\;\text{there is }w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}\text{ such that }w\sim w^{\prime}\,\}
.

As expected from the discussion above, the semantics of systems is
naturally concurrency closed since in a distributed setting
independent events can occur in any order.
Indeed, relation
\sim
can be characterised as follows.

Lemma
.

Given a g-language
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
and two words

w_{1},w_{2}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
,
w_{1}\sim w_{2}
iff

w_{1}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}}}=w_{2}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}}}
for each

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{ptp}}({\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}})
.

Proof.

This follows directly from
[
Gas90
, Proposition 2.2]
.
∎

Therefore we have

Proposition
.

Let
S
be a system. Then
{\llbracket S\rrbracket}
is
concurrency closed.

Proof.

Trivial, since closure under swap does not change the projection by

Section

2
.
∎

The intuition that g-languages, equipped with the projection and
semantic functions of
Section

2
and
Section

2
, do
correspond to a natural syntax and semantics for the abstract notion
of choreography, can be strengthened by showing that these functions
form a Galois connection.

Let us define

\mathbb{G}=\{\,\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}\;\big|\;\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}\text{ is a g-language}\,\}
and

\mathbb{S}=\{\,S\;\big|\;S\text{ is a system}\,\}
.
Moreover, given
S,S^{\prime}\in\mathbb{S}
, we define

S\subseteq S^{\prime}
if
S(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}})\subseteq S^{\prime}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}})
for each
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}
.

Proposition
.

The functions
\_{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}
and
{\llbracket\_\rrbracket}
form a (monotone)
Galois connection between the posets
(\mathbb{G},\subseteq)
and

(\mathbb{S},\subseteq)
, namely,
\_{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}
and
{\llbracket\_\rrbracket}
are
monotone functions such that, given
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}\in\mathbb{G}
and

S\in\mathbb{S}
:

\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}\subseteq S\iff\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}\subseteq{\llbracket S\rrbracket}

Proof.

Note that
(\_){\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}
and
{\llbracket\_\rrbracket}
are trivially monotone by their
definitions.

(
\implies
)
We first observe that

\begin{array}[]{llll}{\llbracket\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}\rrbracket}&=&\{\,w^{\prime}\in\Sigma_{\text{int}}^{\infty}\;\big|\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{ptp}}({w^{\prime}})\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{ptp}}({\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}})\text{ and for all }\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{ptp}}({\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}}),\ w^{\prime}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}}}\,\}\\
&\subseteq&\{\,w^{\prime}\in\Sigma_{\text{int}}^{\infty}\;\big|\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{ptp}}({w^{\prime}})\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{ptp}}({\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}})\text{ and for all }\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{ptp}}({\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}}),\ w^{\prime}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}}}\in S(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}})\,\}\\
&=&{\llbracket S\rrbracket}\end{array}

where the equalities above hold by
Section

2
and the inclusion holds by hypothesis
(
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}\subseteq S
).
Hence, given a word
w\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
, we get

w\in{\llbracket\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}\rrbracket}
by construction and
w\in{\llbracket S\rrbracket}
by the above.

(
\Longleftarrow
) We have to show that

\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}\subseteq S(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}})
for each
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}
. Let hence

w_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}}}
. By definition there is

w\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
with
w{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}}}=w_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}
.
Then
w\in{\llbracket S\rrbracket}
by hypothesis and hence, by

Section

2
,
w{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}}}(=w_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}})\in S(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}})
.
∎

Notice that, by
Section

2
,
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}\subseteq S
can be
understood as “
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
can be realised by
S
” according to the
notion of realisability frequently used in the literature, namely that
all behaviours of the choreography are possible for the system.

A
closure operator
is a function
cl
whose domain and
codomain are ordered sets and such that
cl
is monotone
(
x\leq y\implies{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{cl}}({x})\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{cl}}({y})
), extensive
(
x\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{cl}}({x})
), and idempotent (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{cl}}({x})={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{cl}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{cl}}({x})})
).
It is well-known that, given a Galois connection

(f_{\star},f^{\star})
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{cl}}=f^{\star}\circ f_{\star}
is a closure
operator.
In our setting
{\llbracket\_{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}\rrbracket}
is a closure operator,
hence the above boils down to the following corollary:

Corollary
.

For all g-languages
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L},\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{G}
,

monotonicity:

\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}\subseteq\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}^{\prime}\implies{\llbracket\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}\rrbracket}\subseteq{\llbracket\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}^{\prime}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}\rrbracket}
,

extensiveness:

\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}\subseteq{\llbracket\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}\rrbracket}
,

idempotency:

{\llbracket{\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}}\rrbracket}={\llbracket{\llbracket\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}\rrbracket}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}\rrbracket}
.

As we shall see, extensiveness coincides with completeness
(
Section

3
) and, together with monotonicity, implies

harmonicity
(
Section

4
).

3.
Correctness and Completeness

A g-language specifies the expected communication behaviour of a
system made of several components.

Section

2
formalises such systems in terms of l-languages.
In the present section we deal with properties relating a
communicating system with a specification (i.e., a g-language). In
particular, we first introduce correctness and completeness of a
communicating system with respect a g-language. The latter property
follows by the Galois connection discussed in the previous section.
Instead in order to prove correctness conditions are introduced in
terms of a closure property, CUI; this requires to handle
continuity. We highlight the expressiveness of CUI g-languages by
showing that there exist non-regular CUI g-languages.

Definition

(Correctness and completeness)
.

Let
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
be a g-language.
A system
S
is
correct with respect to
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
if

{\llbracket S\rrbracket}\subseteq\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
and it is
complete with respect
to
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
if
{\llbracket S\rrbracket}\supseteq\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
.

Correctness and completeness are related to existing notions.
For instance, in the literature on multiparty session types (see,
e.g., the survey
[
HLV+16
]
) correctness is analogous to

subject reduction
and completeness to
session fidelity
.

Notice that, by
Section

2
, we can interpret

\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}\subseteq S
as a characterisation for
completeness of
S
with respect to
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
.
Hence, an immediate result of the Galois connection defined in

Section

2
is that any system projected from a g-language is
complete.
In fact, completeness coincides with the extensiveness property of the
closure operator associated to our Galois connection.

Corollary
.

For any g-language
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
,
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}
is complete with
respect to
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
.

It is easy to check that a similar result does not hold for correctness.
If we consider the g-language
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
of
Section

2
, we have that,
as shown in
Section

2
,
\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{w}}}$}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{w}}}$}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{g}}}$}\in{\llbracket\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}\rrbracket}
but
\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{w}}}$}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{w}}}$}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{g}}}$}\not\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
.
That is
{\llbracket\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}\rrbracket}\not\subseteq\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
.

Characterising correctness for projected systems.

Can we identify conditions on g-languages to ensure correctness of
their projections?
The answer to this question is positive since correctness can be
characterised as a closure property.

Definition

(CUI)
.

A g-language
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
is
closed under unknown information
, in
symbols
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{cui}}({\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}})
, if for all finite words

w_{1}\cat\alpha,w_{2}\cat\alpha\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
with the
same final interaction
\alpha=\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}$}\in\Sigma_{\text{int}}
we have

w\cat\alpha\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
for all
w\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
such
that
{w}\downarrow_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}={w_{1}}\downarrow_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}
and

{w}\downarrow_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}={w_{2}}\downarrow_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}
.

Intuitively, participants cannot distinguish words with the same
projection on their role.
Hence, if two participants
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}
find words
w_{1}
and

w_{2}
compatible with another word
w
, and interaction

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}

can occur after both
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
,
then it should be enabled also after
w
.
Indeed,
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}
cannot know whether the current word is
w
or

w_{1}
and, likewise,
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}
cannot tell apart
w
and

w_{2}
.
Hence,
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
should encompass
w
because, after the
execution of
w
,
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}
are willing to take

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}

, which
can thus happen at the system level.
Closure under unknown information (CUI for short) lifts this
requirement at the level of g-language.

Example
.

The language
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
in
Section

2
is not CUI because it
contains the words

w_{1}\cat\alpha=\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{w}}}$}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{g}}}$}\qquad w_{2}\cat\alpha=\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{w}}}$}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{g}}}$}\qquad\text{and}\qquad w=\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{w}}}$}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{w}}}$}

and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}
cannot distinguish between
w_{1}
and
w
while

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}
cannot distinguish between
w_{2}
and
w
;
nonetheless

w\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{g}}}$}=\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{w}}}$}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{w}}}$}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{g}}}$}\not\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
.
Notice that, as shown after
Section

3

\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}\not\supseteq{\llbracket\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}\rrbracket}
.

\diamond

The language in
Section

3
is not the semantics of any system,
in fact languages obtained as semantics of a communicating system are
always CUI.

Proposition

(Semantics is CUI)
.

For all systems
S
,
{\llbracket S\rrbracket}
is CUI.

Proof.

Let
S
be a system over
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}
.
In order to show closure of
{\llbracket S\rrbracket}
under unknown information, let
us take words

w_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}$},\ w_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}$},\ w^{\prime}\in{\llbracket S\rrbracket}
, such that

\displaystyle w_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}}}=w^{\prime}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad w_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}}}}=w^{\prime}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}}}}

By
Section

3
, we have to show that

w^{\prime}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}$}\in{\llbracket S\rrbracket}
.
This in turn, by definition of synchronous semantics, amounts to
show that
(w^{\prime}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}$}){\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}\in S({\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}})
for each

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}
.
If
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}\neq\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}},{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}
then

(w^{\prime}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}$}){\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}=w^{\prime}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}\in S({\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}})

since
w^{\prime}\in{\llbracket S\rrbracket}
.
Otherwise
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}\in\{\,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}},{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}\,\}
; hence, by hypothesis and
definition of projection we have

(w^{\prime}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}$}){\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}}}=w_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}}}\cat\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{!}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}{}$}\in S(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}})\quad\text{and}\quad(w^{\prime}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}$}){\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}}}}=w_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}}}}\cat\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}{}$}\in S({\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}})

where the last two equalities hold by hypothesis.
∎

The next property connects finite and infinite words in a language; it
corresponds to the closure under the limit operation used in

\omega
-languages
[
Eil76
,
Sta97
]
.

Definition

(Continuity)
.

A language
L
on an alphabet
\Sigma
is
continuous
if

z\in L
for all
z\in\Sigma^{\omega}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{pref}}({z})\cap L

is infinite.

This notion of continuity, besides being quite natural, is the most
suitable for our purposes among the possible
ones
[
Red86
]
.
Intuitively, a language
L
is continuous if an
\omega
-word is in

L
when infinitely many of its approximants (i.e., finite prefixes)
are in
L
.
A g-language
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
is
standard or continuous
(sc-language, for
short) if either
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}\subseteq\Sigma_{\text{int}}^{\star}
or
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
is
continuous.
Notice that, for prefix-closed languages, for all

z\in\Sigma^{\omega}
we have that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{pref}}({z})\cap L
is infinite
iff
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{pref}}({z})\subseteq L
.

Closure under unknown information characterises correct
projected systems.

Theorem

(Characterisation of correctness)
.

If
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}
is correct with respect to
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{cui}}({\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}})
holds.
If
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
is an sc-language and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{cui}}({\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}})
then
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}

is correct with respect to
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
.

Proof.

We prove the first implication.
In order to show closure of
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
under unknown information, let
us take words

w_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}=w^{\prime}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}$},\ w_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}=w^{\prime}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}$},\ w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
, such that

\displaystyle w^{\prime}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}}}=w^{\prime}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad w^{\prime}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}}}}=w^{\prime}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}}}}

(1)

By
Section

3
, we have to show that

w^{\prime}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}$}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
.
Thanks to correctness, it is enough to show that

w^{\prime}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}$}\in{\llbracket\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}\rrbracket}
.
This in turn, by definition of synchronous semantics, amounts to
show that

v_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}=(w^{\prime}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}$}){\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}
for each
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}
.
If
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}\neq\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}},{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}
then

v_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}=(w^{\prime}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}$}){\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}=w^{\prime}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}
.
Otherwise
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}\in\{\,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}},{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}\,\}
; we consider only the case

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}
since the other case is analogous.
We have

\begin{array}[]{lcll}v_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}&=&(w^{\prime}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}$}){\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}}}\\
&=&w^{\prime}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}}}\cat(\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}$}){\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}}}&\text{(by def. of projection)}\\
&=&w^{\prime}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}}}\cat(\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}$}){\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}}}&\text{(by (\ref{eq:wawb}))}\\
&=&(w^{\prime}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}$}){\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}&\text{(by def. of projection and $w^{\prime}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}$}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}$)}\end{array}

as required.

We now prove the second implication.
By
Section

3
, we have to show that

{\llbracket\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}\rrbracket}\subseteq\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
; we proceed by
contradiction.
Fix a word
w\in{\llbracket\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}\rrbracket}\setminus\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
.
The only possible cases are:

\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}\subseteq\Sigma_{\text{int}}^{\star}
By
Section

2
,

{\llbracket\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}\rrbracket}
does not contain infinite
g-words.
Hence,
w
is finite and we can take its longest prefix

w^{\prime}
that belongs to
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
.
Let
\alpha=\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}$}
be the interaction immediately following

w^{\prime}
in
w
.
We can choose
w_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}},w_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
such that

w_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}=w{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}
and

w_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}=w{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}
.
(Recall that by
Section

2
for each
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}

there is a word
w_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
such that

w{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}=w_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}
.)
Take the shortest prefixes
w^{\prime}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}
and
w^{\prime}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}
of

w_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}
and
w_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}
respectively such that

w^{\prime}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}=(w^{\prime}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}$}){\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad w^{\prime}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}=(w^{\prime}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}$}){\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}

It is then easy to check that
w^{\prime}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}
and
w^{\prime}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}

have necessarily the following shapes

w^{\prime}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}=\tilde{w}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}$}\qquad\qquad w^{\prime}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}=\tilde{w}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}$}

where

•

\tilde{w}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}},\tilde{w}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
, since

\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
is prefix-closed;

•

\tilde{w}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}=w^{\prime}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}
and

\tilde{w}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}=w^{\prime}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}
.

Now, by
w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
and the definition of CUI, we infer that
w^{\prime}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}$}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}

against the hypothesis that
w^{\prime}
was the longest such prefix
of
w
.

\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}\nsubseteq\Sigma_{\text{int}}^{\star}
If the set

\widehat{\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}}=\{\,w^{\prime}\in\Sigma_{\text{int}}^{\star}\;\big|\;w^{\prime}\preceq w\text{ and }w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}\,\}
is finite then we
take the longest g-word in
\widehat{\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}}
and the proof is as in
the previous case. Otherwise we have a contradiction because, by continuity,

w\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
.
∎

Notice that CUI is defined in terms of g-languages only, hence
checking CUI does not require to build the corresponding system.
This allows us to study CUI on specific classes of g-languages.
For instance, we can show that CUI is decidable on a class of languages
accepted by Büchi automata (cf.
Section

7
).
An interesting observation is that strengthening the precondition of

Section

3
with the additional requirement

w_{1}=w_{2}
would invalidate
Section

3
.
Indeed,
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}\cup\{\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{g}}}$}\}
with
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
the
language in
Section

2
would become CUI but not correct.
The next example shows that the continuity condition in

Section

3
is necessary for languages containing infinite
g-words.

Example

(Continuity matters)
.

The CUI language

\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{pref}}({{\bigcup_{i\geq 0}\{\,\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{l}}}$}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{n}}}$}\cat(\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{D}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{n}}}$})^{i}\,\}}\cup\{\,\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{r}}}$}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{n}}}$}\cat(\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{D}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{n}}}$})^{\omega}\,\}})

does contain an infinite word but it is not continuous.
The projection of
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
is not correct because its semantics
contains the g-word

\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{l}}}$}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{n}}}$}\cat(\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{D}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{n}}}$})^{\omega}
.
This word, in fact, does not belong to
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
since the
projections of
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{D}}
can exchange infinitely many messages

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{n}}
due to the infinite g-word of
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
regardless whether

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}
exchange
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{l}}
or
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{r}}
.

\diamond

Notice that, since
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}\subseteq{\llbracket\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}\rrbracket}
always
holds,
Section

3
implies that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{cui}}({\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}})

characterises the languages
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
such that

\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}={\llbracket\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}\rrbracket}
.
Besides, the following corollary descends from
Section

3
.

Corollary
.

If
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
is an sc-language,
{\llbracket\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}\rrbracket}
is the smallest
CUI sc-language containing
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
.

Proof.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{cl}}({\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}})={{\llbracket\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}\rrbracket}}
.
Given an sc-language
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{cui}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{cl}}({\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}})})
holds by

Section

3
. Moreover, it is not difficult to check that
if
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
is an sc-language, so is
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{cl}}({\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}})
.

\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{cl}}({\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}})
holds by extensiveness of
cl
. Now,
in order to show that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{cl}}({\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}})
is smaller or equal than any CUI
sc-language containing
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
, let us consider any sc-language
\tilde{\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{cui}}({\tilde{\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}}})
and

\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}\subseteq\tilde{\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}}
. By monotonicity of
cl
, we have
that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{cl}}({\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}})\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{cl}}({\tilde{\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}}})
and, by

Section

3
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{cl}}({\tilde{\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}}})=\widehat{\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}}
. So

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{cl}}({\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}})\subseteq\tilde{\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}}
.
∎

CUI ensures that continuous g-languages are concurrency closed.

Proposition
.

If
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
is an sc-language and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{cui}}({\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}})
, then
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
is
concurrency closed.

Proof.

Thanks to
Section

3

\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}={\llbracket\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}\rrbracket}
,
hence it is concurrency closed thanks to
Section

2
.
∎

Hence, an sc-language cannot be CUI unless it is
concurrency closed.

As recalled before, in many choreographic formalisms (such
as
[
BDCLT21
,
HLV+16
,
CDYP16
,
BBO12
,
FBS04
]
) the correctness
and completeness of a projected system, namely

\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}={\llbracket\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}\rrbracket}{}
(together with some forms of
liveness and deadlock-freedom properties), is guaranteed by

well-branchedness
conditions.
Most of such conditions guarantee, informally speaking, that
participants reach consensus on which branch to take when choices
arise.
For instance, a well-branchedness condition could be that, at each
choice, there is a unique participant deciding the branch to follow
during a computation and that such participant informs each other
participant.
Such a condition is actually not
needed to prove
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}={\llbracket\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}\rrbracket}{}
, as shown by the example below.

Example
.

The g-language
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
of
Section

2
is CUI, without being
well-branched in the above sense.
Indeed, after the interaction

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{w}}}

, there is a
branching in the projected system, since both the interactions

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{w}}}

and

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{g}}}

can be performed.
However, these interactions do not have the same sender.

A key merit of our model is its generality and expressiveness.
We show this with an example of a non-regular CUI g-language whose
projected system is correct and complete by
Section

3
and

Section

3
, respectively.

Example

(A non-regular CUI g-language)
.

We consider a task dispatching service where, as soon as a

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{S}}
erver communicates its
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}
vailability, a

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{D}}
ispatcher sends a
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{t}}
ask to
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{S}}
.
The server either processes the task directly and sends back the
resulting
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{d}}
ata to
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{D}}
or sends the task to participant

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{H}}
for some pre-processing, aiming at resuming it later on.
Indeed, after communicating a result to
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{D}}
, the server can

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{r}}
esume a previous task (if any) from
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{H}}
, process it,
and send the result to
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{D}}
.
The server eventually stops by sending
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{s}}
to both
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{D}}

and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{H}}
; this can happen only when all dispatched tasks have
been processed.
The task dispatching scenario above is faithfully captured by the
g-language
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
obtained by prefix-closing the (non-regular)
language generated by the following context-free grammar.

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle\ ::=\

\displaystyle L^{\prime}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{D}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{s}}}$}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{H}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{s}}}$}

\displaystyle L^{\prime}

\displaystyle\ ::=\

\displaystyle\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{D}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}}$}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{D}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{S}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{t}}}$}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{H}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{t}}}$}\cat L^{\prime}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{H}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{r}}}$}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{H}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{S}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{r}}}$}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{D}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{d}}}$}\cat L^{\prime}\;\ \big|\ \;\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{D}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{a}}}$}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{D}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{S}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{t}}}$}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{D}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{d}}}$}\cat L^{\prime}\;\ \big|\ \;\varepsilon

Since
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{S}}
is involved in all the interactions of
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
, for
each pair of words
w,w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
:

w{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{S}}}}=w^{\prime}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{S}}}}\textit{iff }w=w^{\prime}
.
Now, if
w_{1}\,\alpha,w_{2}\,\alpha,w\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}

satisfy the required conditions for CUI then either

w_{1}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{S}}}}=w{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{S}}}}
or

w_{2}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{S}}}}=w{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{S}}}}
, since

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{S}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{ptp}}({\alpha})
.
Hence
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{cui}}({\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}})
trivially holds.

\diamond

The language in
Section

3
is non-regular since it has the
same structure of a language of well-balanced parenthesis.
Remarkably, this implies that the g-language cannot be expressed in
most of the other choreographic models in the literature.
There are models going beyond regular languages for both the binary
(e.g.,
[
Dar14
,
TV16
]
) and the multi-party case
(e.g.,
[
JY20
]
).
These approaches are based on process algebraic methods.
An interesting future research direction would be to study whether the
multi-party models give rise to CUI and branch-aware languages.
The argument used to show
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{cui}}({\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}})
in
Section

3
proves the
following.

Proposition
.

If there exists a participant involved in all the interactions of a
g-language
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{cui}}({\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}})
holds.

4.
Communication Properties

Besides correctness and completeness, other properties could be of
interest for message-passing systems.
For instance, one would like to ensure that participants eventually
interact, if they are willing to.
More generally, we are interested in some relations between the
interactions in a system and the communication actions of its
participants.
We consider a number of properties, defined as follows.

Harmonicity
(HA) requires that each sequence of communications
that a participant is able to perform can be executed in some
computation of the system.

Definition

(Harmonicity)
.

A system
S
on
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}
is
harmonic
if

S(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}})\subseteq{\llbracket S\rrbracket}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}}}
for each participant

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}
.

The remaining communication properties rely on the notion of maximal
computation (cf. beginning of
Section

2
on page

2
).

Lock-freedom
(LF) requires that if a participant has pending
communications to make on an ongoing computation, then there is a
continuation of the computation involving that participant.

Definition

(Lock-freedom)
.

A system
S
on
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}
is
lock free
if, for each finite
word
w\in{\llbracket S\rrbracket}
and participant
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}
, if

w{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}}}
is not maximal in
S(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}})
then there is a
word
w^{\prime}
such that
ww^{\prime}\in{\llbracket S\rrbracket}
and

w^{\prime}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}}}\neq\varepsilon
.

Strong lock-freedom
(SLF) requires that if a participant has
pending communications to make on an ongoing computation, then each
maximal continuation of the computation involves that participant.

Definition

(Strong lock-freedom)
.

A system
S
on
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}
is
strongly lock free
if, for
each finite word
w\in{\llbracket S\rrbracket}
and participant

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}
, if
w{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}}}
is not maximal in

S(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}})
then for each word
w^{\prime}
such that
ww^{\prime}

is maximal in
{\llbracket S\rrbracket}
we have

w^{\prime}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}}}\neq\varepsilon
.

Starvation-freedom
(SF) requires that if a participant has
pending communications to make on an ongoing computation, then each
infinite continuation of the computation involves that participant.

Definition

(Starvation-freedom)
.

A system
S
on
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}
is
starvation free
if, for each
finite word
w\in{\llbracket S\rrbracket}
and participant

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}
, if
w{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}}}
is not maximal in

S(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}})
then
w^{\prime}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}}}\neq\varepsilon
for each
infinite word
w^{\prime}
such that

ww^{\prime}\in{\llbracket S\rrbracket}
.

Deadlock-freedom
(DF) requires that in all completed
computations each participant has no pending actions.

Definition

(Deadlock-freedom)
.

A system
S
on
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}
is
deadlock free
if, for each
finite and maximal word
w\in{\llbracket S\rrbracket}
and participant

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}
,
w{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}
is maximal in
S(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}})
.

Barred for harmonicity, these properties appear in the literature
under different names in various contexts.
For instance, the notion of lock-freedom in
[
BDCLT21
]
corresponds
to ours, which in turn corresponds to the notion of liveness
in
[
LNTY17
,
KS10
]
in a channel-based synchronous
communication setting.
Likewise, the notion of strong lock-freedom in
[
SD19
]

corresponds to ours and, under fair scheduling, to the notion of
lock-freedom in
[
Kob02
]
.
As a final example, the definition of deadlock-freedom
in its (equivalent) contrapositive form, coincides with the notion
of progress as defined for synchronous processes
in
[
Pad13
,
GJP+19
]
.
Harmonicity, introduced in the present paper, assures that no
behaviour of a participant can be taken out from a system without
affecting the overall behaviour of the system itself.
Notice that the inverse of harmonicity,

{\llbracket S\rrbracket}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}}}\subseteq S(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}})
, holds by construction.

The next proposition highlights the relations among our
properties.

Proposition
.

The following relations hold among the properties in

Sections

4
,
4
,
4
,
4
and
4

LF
SLF
DF
HA
SF
/
/

where implication does not hold in any direction between properties
connected by dashed lines.
Moreover,
\textsf{DF}\wedge\textsf{SF}\Leftrightarrow\textsf{SLF}
.

Proof.

SLF
\implies
LF
Let
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}
,

w\in{\llbracket S\rrbracket}
be finite, and
w{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}
not to
be maximal in
S(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}})
. By
SLF
we can infer that also

w
is not maximal in
{\llbracket S\rrbracket}
. Otherwise, for

w^{\prime}=\varepsilon
, we would have
w\cat w^{\prime}
to be
maximal in
{\llbracket S\rrbracket}
and, by
SLF
, we would get

\varepsilon=w^{\prime}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}\neq\varepsilon
. Contradiction. So, if

w
is not maximal in
{\llbracket S\rrbracket}
, there exists

w^{\prime}\neq\varepsilon
such that
w\cat w^{\prime}
is maximal
in
{\llbracket S\rrbracket}
. We get hence immediately

w^{\prime}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}\neq\varepsilon
by
SLF
.

LF
\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.20757pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\implies}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.20757pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\implies}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.8375pt\kern-3.04367pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\implies}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.3125pt\kern-2.49922pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\implies}}}
SLF

Let us take the communicating system
S=(\mathbb{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}})_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}\in\{\,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}},{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}\,\}}
,
where

\mathbb{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{pref}}({\{\,\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{!}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}{}$}\cat\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{!}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}{}$},\,\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{!}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}{}$}\cat\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{!}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}{}$}\,\}})\quad\mathbb{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{pref}}({\{\,\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}{}$}\cat\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}{}$}\,\}})\quad\mathbb{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{pref}}({\{\,\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}{}$}\,\}})

and whose synchronous semantics is

{\llbracket S\rrbracket}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{pref}}({\{\,\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}}$}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}}$},\,\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}}$}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}}$}\,\}})

S
is lock-free, but not strongly lock-free. In fact, for

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}}

and
{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}
, we have that

\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}}$}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}}$}
is maximal, but

(\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}}$}){\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}=\varepsilon
.

LF
\implies
DF
Let us assume
S
to be
lock-free. By contradiction let us assume
S
not to be
deadlock-free.
Then there is a finite and maximal word
w\in{\llbracket S\rrbracket}

and a participant
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}
such that
w{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}
is not maximal
in
S(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}})
.
Since
S
is lock-free, by definition, there is a word
w^{\prime}

such that
ww^{\prime}\in{\llbracket S\rrbracket}
and

w^{\prime}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}}}\neq\varepsilon
.
Hence
w^{\prime}\neq\varepsilon
and therefore
w
is not
maximal in
S
, contrary to our assumption.

DF
\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.20757pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\implies}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.20757pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\implies}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.8375pt\kern-3.04367pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\implies}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.3125pt\kern-2.49922pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\implies}}}
LF

Let us take

\mathbb{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{pref}}({\{\,(\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{!}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}{}$})^{\omega}\,\}})\quad\mathbb{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{pref}}({\{\,(\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}{}$})^{\omega}\,\}})\quad\mathbb{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{pref}}({\{\,\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}{}$}\,\}})

and consider the communicating system

S=(\mathbb{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}})_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}\in\{\,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}},{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}\,\}}\quad\text{whose synchronous semantics is}\quad{\llbracket S\rrbracket}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{pref}}({\{\,(\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}}$})^{\omega}\,\}})

It is immediate to check that
S
is vacuously deadlock free,
since there is no finite maximal word in
S
.
However,
S
is not lock-free. It is enough to take

, which is finite in
{\llbracket S\rrbracket}
and such that

\mathbb{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}}

However there is no
w^{\prime}
such that

w\cat w^{\prime}\in{\llbracket S\rrbracket}
and

w^{\prime}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}}}\neq\varepsilon
.

DF
\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.20757pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\implies}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.20757pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\implies}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.8375pt\kern-3.04367pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\implies}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.3125pt\kern-2.49922pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\implies}}}
SF
Let

\displaystyle\mathbb{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{pref}}({\{\,(\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{!}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}{}$})^{\omega},\ \scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{!}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}{}$}\cat(\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{!}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}{}$})^{\omega}\,\}})

\displaystyle\mathbb{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{pref}}({\{\,(\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}{}$})^{\omega}\,\}})

\displaystyle\mathbb{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{pref}}({\{\,(\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}{}$})^{\omega}\,\}})

and consider the communicating system

S=(\mathbb{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}})_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}\in\{\,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}},{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}\,\}}

whose synchronous semantics is

{\llbracket S\rrbracket}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{pref}}({\{\,(\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}}$})^{\omega},\ \scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}}$}\cat(\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}}$})^{\omega}\,\}})

It is immediate to check that

.

is not starvation-free.
It is enough to take

we have that
w\cat w^{\prime}\in{\llbracket S\rrbracket}
and

\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.20757pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\implies}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.20757pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\implies}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.8375pt\kern-3.04367pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\implies}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.3125pt\kern-2.49922pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\implies}}}
DF

Let

\mathbb{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}=\{\,\varepsilon,\ \scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{!}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}{}$}\,\}\quad\mathbb{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}=\{\,\varepsilon,\ \scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}{}$},\ \scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{!}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}{}$}\,\}\quad\mathbb{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}}=\{\,\varepsilon,\ \scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}{}$}\,\}

S=(\mathbb{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}})_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}\in\{\,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}},{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}\,\}}\quad\text{whose synchronous semantics is}\quad{\llbracket S\rrbracket}=\{\,\varepsilon,\ \scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}}$},\ \scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}}$}\,\}

.

is not maximal in
