It is not difficult to show that branch-awareness actually
characterises
SLF
for systems obtained by projecting CUI
languages.

Proposition

(Branch-awareness characterises
SLF
)
.

A CUI g-language
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
is branch-aware iff
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}
is strongly lock-free.

Proof.

Necessity follows from
Section

5
while
for sufficiency we reason by contraposition.
Assume
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
not to be branch-aware.
Then, by
Section

5
there exist a participant
{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}
and two
maximal words in
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
such that their projections on
{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}

differ and
either
{w_{1}}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}\prec{w_{2}}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}

or
{w_{2}}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}\prec{w_{1}}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}
.
Let us consider the first case (the second is analogous).
Let now
w^{\prime}_{1}
and
w^{\prime}_{2}
be the maximal prefixes (hence
finite) of, respectively,
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
such that

{w^{\prime}_{1}}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}={w^{\prime}_{2}}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}
.
We distinguish the following two cases.

w^{\prime}_{1}=w_{1}
In such a case we get that

\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}
is not deadlock-free, since, for the finite
maximal
w
we have that
{w_{1}}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}
is not
maximal. So, by
Section

5
,
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}
is
not strongly lock-free as well.

w^{\prime}_{1}\prec w_{1}

In such a case we get that
w^{\prime}_{1}
is a non-maximal finite word
such that
w_{1}=w^{\prime}_{1}\cat w^{\prime\prime}_{1}
is maximal,

{w_{1}}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}
is non maximal and

{w^{\prime\prime}_{1}}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}=\varepsilon
, that is
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}
is
not strongly lock-free.
∎

6.
CFSMs and Choreography Languages

Communicating finite-state machines
(CFSMs) have been
introduced in
[
BZ83
]
as a convenient model to analyse
message-passing protocols.
Basically, a CFSM is a finite-state automaton (FSA), defined below,
whose transitions are actions.

Definition

(Finite state automaton (FSA))
.

A
finite state automaton
(FSA) is a tuple

A=\langle{\mathcal{S},q_{0},\mathbf{\Lambda},\to}\rangle
where

•

\mathcal{S}
is a finite set of states (ranged over by
q,s,\ldots
) and
q_{0}\in\mathcal{S}
is the
initial state
;

•

\mathbf{\Lambda}
is a finite set of labels (ranged over by

{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{l}},\lambda,\ldots
);

•

\to\subseteq\mathcal{S}\times\mathbf{\Lambda}\times\mathcal{S}
is a set of transitions.

We use the usual notation
q_{1}\xrightarrow{\lambda}q_{2}
for the transition
(q_{1},\lambda,q_{2})\in\xrightarrow{}
, and

q_{1}\xrightarrow{}q_{2}
when there exists
\lambda
such that

q_{1}\xrightarrow{\lambda}q_{2}
, as well as
\xrightarrow{}^{*}
for the reflexive and transitive
closure of
\xrightarrow{}
.

The set of
reachable states
of
A
is

\mathsf{R}({A})=\{\,q\;\big|\;q_{0}\xrightarrow{}^{\star}q\,\}
.

Remark
.

Our definition of FSA omits the set of
accepting
states since
we consider only FSAs where each state is accepting.

\diamond

Following the assumption in
Section

6
, we define the language

\mathbf{L}(A)
as the union of the finite words accepted by
A
in the
classical sense and the infinite words accepted by
A
considered as
Büchi automaton
2
2

2

A Büchi automaton accepts an infinite
word
z
when it traverses infinitely often elements of the
set of accepting states while consuming the symbols of
z
.

where all states are accepting.

A CFSM is an FSA labelled in
\Sigma_{\text{act}}\cup\{\,\varepsilon\,\}
, where

\varepsilon
is not a symbol in
\Sigma_{\text{act}}
and it overloads the
notation for the empty string to represent internal transitions of
CFSMs as usual in automata theory.

Remark
.

FSAs, and consequently CFSMs, can be deterministic or not.
Deterministic FSAs have no label
\varepsilon
, and transitions from
the same state are pairwise different.
Given a non-deterministic FSA one can build a deterministic FSA
generating the same language.
We will assume that each CFSM is deterministic since we are
interested in languages and non-deterministic CFSMs can be
determinised while preserving their language.

Notice that it is necessary to adopt finer notions of equivalence
such as bisimilarity
[
Par81
]
in order to tell apart
deterministic FSAs from non-deterministic ones.
It could be interesting to investigate the possibility of including
the notion of nondeterminism in FCLs in the future.

\diamond

A CFSM is
local
to a participant
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}
(
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}
-local for short) if
all its transitions have subject
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}
; we will consider communicating
systems where the behaviour of each participant
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}
is specified by an

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}
-local CFSM.

Formally, given a finite set
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}\subseteq\mathfrak{P}
of
participants, a
system of CFSMs
is a map

(M_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}})_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}}
assigning an
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}
-local CFSM
M_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}

to each participant
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}
such that any participant
occurring in a label of a transition of
M_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}
is in
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}
.

The synchronous behaviour of a system of CFSMs

(M_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}})_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}}
has been defined in
[
BLT20
]
as any
FSA where states are maps assigning a state in
M_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}
to each

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}
and transitions are labelled by interactions.
Intuitively, given a configuration
s
, if
M_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}
and

M_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}
have respectively transitions

s(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}})\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{!}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}{}$}}q_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}^{\prime}
and
s({\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}})\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}{}$}}q_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}^{\prime}

then

s\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}$}}s[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\mapsto q_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}^{\prime},{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}\mapsto q_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}^{\prime}]
,
where
f[x\mapsto y]
denotes the update of
f
on
x
with
y
.
The next definition is a slightly different version of the one
in
[
BLT20
]
, where the semantics of a system of CFSMs
\mathbf{S}
is
the FSA
(\hskip-2.2pt|\mathbf{S}|\hskip-2.2pt)
defined below rather than its language as in our
case.

Definition

(Synchronous semantics of systems of CFSMs)
.

Let
\mathbf{S}=(M_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}})_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}}
be a system of CFSMs where

M_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}=\langle{\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}},q_{0\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}},\Sigma_{\text{act}},\to_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}\rangle
for
each participant
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}
.
A
synchronous configuration
for
\mathbf{S}
is a map

s=(q_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}})_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}}
assigning a
local state

q_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}
to each
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}
.

The
synchronous semantics
of
\mathbf{S}
is the g-language

\mathbf{L}((\hskip-2.2pt|\mathbf{S}|\hskip-2.2pt))
where

(\hskip-2.2pt|\mathbf{S}|\hskip-2.2pt)=\langle{\mathcal{S},s_{0},\Sigma_{\text{int}},\to}\rangle
is defined as
follows:

•

\mathcal{S}
is the set of configurations of
\mathbf{S}
, as defined
above, and
s_{0}:\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\mapsto q_{0\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}
for each
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}
is the
initial
configuration of
\mathcal{S}

•

\to
is the set of transitions

s_{1}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}$}}s_{2}
such that

–

s_{1}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}})\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{!}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}{}$}}s_{2}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}})
in
M_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}
and

s_{1}({\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}})\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}{}$}}s_{2}({\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}})
in
M_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}
, and

–

for all
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}\setminus\{\,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}},{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}\,\}
,

s_{1}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}})=s_{2}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}})
.

As we will see in
Section

7
, the synchronous
semantics in
Section

6
is a
choreography automaton

[
BLT20
]
.
Note that this is not the case for the asynchronous semantics of
communicating systems which is in general a transition system with
infinitely many configurations.

An immediate relation between systems of CFSMs and our communicating
systems is that, given a system of CFSMs

\mathbf{S}=(M_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}})_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}}
, we can define the
abstract system corresponding to
\mathbf{S}
as

\hat{\mathbf{S}}=(\mathbf{L}(M_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}))_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}}
.
Unsurprisingly, the semantics of
\mathbf{S}
and
\hat{\mathbf{S}}
do coincide.

Proposition
.

For all systems
\mathbf{S}=(M_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}})_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}}
of
CFSMs,
\mathbf{L}((\hskip-2.2pt|\mathbf{S}|\hskip-2.2pt))={\llbracket\hat{\mathbf{S}}\rrbracket}
.

Proof.

The proof shows the commutativity of the following
diagram

\mathbb{S}
\mathfrak{L}
\mathfrak{C}
\mathbb{G}
(\hskip-2.2pt|\_|\hskip-2.2pt)
\hat{\_}
\mathbf{L}(\_)
{\llbracket\_\rrbracket}

where
\mathbb{S}
is the set of systems of CFSMs,
\mathfrak{L}
is
the set of communicating systems (cf.
Section

2
),

\mathfrak{C}
is the set of c-automata, and
\mathbb{G}
is the
set of global languages.

Let

M_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}={\langle{\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}},{q_{0}}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}},\Sigma_{\text{act}},\to_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}\rangle}
and let

(\hskip-2.2pt|\mathbf{S}|\hskip-2.2pt)=\langle{\mathcal{S},s_{0},\Sigma_{\text{int}},\to}\rangle
where

\langle{\mathcal{S},s_{0},\Sigma_{\text{int}},\to}\rangle
is as in

Section

6
.
We prove the equality by proving separately the following two
inclusions, for all
\mathbf{S}
.

\mathbf{L}((\hskip-2.2pt|\mathbf{S}|\hskip-2.2pt))\subseteq{\llbracket\hat{\mathbf{S}}\rrbracket}

By definition of
\hat{\mathbf{S}}
, it is enough to show that, for any

w\in\Sigma_{\text{int}}^{\infty}
, if
w\in\mathbf{L}((\hskip-2.2pt|\mathbf{S}|\hskip-2.2pt))
,
then for any
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}
, we have that

w{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}\in\mathbf{L}(M_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}})
.
Let
w\in\mathbf{L}((\hskip-2.2pt|\mathbf{S}|\hskip-2.2pt))
and proceed by coinduction on
the paths of machine
(\hskip-2.2pt|\mathbf{S}|\hskip-2.2pt)
.

If
w=\varepsilon
the thesis follows immediately.
Otherwise, let
w=(\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}$})\cat w^{\prime}
.
By definition of recognised word, there is
s_{1}\in\mathcal{S}
such
that
s_{0}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}$}}s_{1}
and
w^{\prime}
is recognised
by the automaton
\langle{\mathcal{S},s_{1},\Sigma_{\text{int}},\to}\rangle
.

Necessarily, by
Section

6
,

(1)

s_{0}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}})\,\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{!}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}{}$}}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}\,s_{1}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}})
and

s_{0}({\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}})\,\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}{}$}}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}\,s_{1}({\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}})
, and

(2)

for all
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}\neq\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}},{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}
,

s_{1}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}})=s_{0}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}})
.

It follows that
w^{\prime}\in\mathbf{L}(\mathbf{S}^{\prime})
where

\mathbf{S}^{\prime}=({\langle{\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}},s_{1}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}),\Sigma_{\text{act}},\to_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}\rangle})_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}\in{\{\,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}},{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}\,\}}}\cup(M_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}})_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}\in{\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}\setminus\{\,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}},{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}\,\}}}
.
The thesis hence follows by coinduction, since

w{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}}}=(\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{!}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}{}$})\cat w^{\prime}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}}}
,

w{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}}}}=(\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}{}$})\cat w^{\prime}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}}}}
and, for each

{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}\in{\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}\setminus\{\,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}},{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}\,\}}}
,

w{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}=w^{\prime}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}
.

{\llbracket\hat{\mathbf{S}}\rrbracket}\subseteq\mathbf{L}((\hskip-2.2pt|\mathbf{S}|\hskip-2.2pt)
)

For this case we have to prove that for all

w\in\Sigma_{\text{int}}^{\infty}
, if
w\in{\llbracket\hat{\mathbf{S}}\rrbracket}
then

w\in\mathbf{L}((\hskip-2.2pt|\mathbf{S}|\hskip-2.2pt))
.
Let
w\in{\llbracket\hat{\mathbf{S}}\rrbracket}
and proceed by coinduction.
If
w=\varepsilon
the thesis follows immediately.
Otherwise, let
w=(\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}$})\cat w^{\prime}
.
Since
w{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}}}=(\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{!}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}{}$})\cat w^{\prime}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}}}
,

w{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}}}}=(\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}{}$})\cat w^{\prime}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}}}}
and, for each

{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}\in{\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}\setminus\{\,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}},{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}\,\}}}
,

w{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}=w^{\prime}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}
, and since, for each

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}
, we have

w{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}\in\mathbf{L}(M_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}})
, it follows, by
definition of recognised word and by
Section

2
, that

w^{\prime}\in{\llbracket\hat{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}\rrbracket}
, with

\hat{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}=(\mathbf{L}({\langle{\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}},s_{1}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}),\Sigma_{\text{act}},\to_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}\rangle}))_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}\in{\{\,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}},{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}\,\}}}\cup(\mathbf{L}(M_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}))_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}\in{\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}\setminus\{\,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}},{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}\,\}}}
,
where

(1)

s_{0}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}})\,\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{!}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}{}$}}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}\,s_{1}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}})
and

s_{0}({\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}})\,\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}{}$}}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}\,s_{1}({\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}})
, and

(2)

for all
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}\neq\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}},{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}
,

s_{1}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}})=s_{0}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}})
.

The thesis hence follows by coinduction.
∎

Notice that the proof of
Section

6
does not require CFSMs to
be deterministic; indeed, the result above holds for non-deterministic
CFSMs too.

The communication properties of a system of CFSMs
\mathbf{S}
on
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}

considered in
[
BLT20
]
are liveness, lock-freedom, and
deadlock-freedom.
Intuitively

•

\mathbf{S}
is
live
when each reachable configuration where a
participant
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}
can execute a communication has a
continuation where
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}
is involved;

•

\mathbf{S}
is
lock-free
when in all computations starting
from a reachable configuration where a participant
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}

can execute,
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}
is involved;

•

\mathbf{S}
is
deadlock-free
if in none of its reachable
configurations without outgoing transitions there exists

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}
willing to communicate.

The next definition formalises properties of systems of CFSMs.

Definition

(Communication properties of systems of CFSMs
[
BLT20
]
)
.

Let
\mathbf{S}=(M_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}})_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}}
be a system of CFSMs.

•

Liveness
:
\mathbf{S}
is
live
if for each
configuration
s\in\mathsf{R}({(\hskip-2.2pt|\mathbf{S}|\hskip-2.2pt)})
and each

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}
such that
s(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}})
has some outgoing
transition in
M_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}
, there exists a run of
(\hskip-2.2pt|\mathbf{S}|\hskip-2.2pt)

from
s
including a transition involving
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}
.

•

Lock freedom
: a configuration

s\in\mathsf{R}({(\hskip-2.2pt|\mathbf{S}|\hskip-2.2pt)})
is a
lock
if there is

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}
with an outgoing transition from
s(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}})
in

M_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}
and there exists a run of
(\hskip-2.2pt|\mathbf{S}|\hskip-2.2pt)
starting
from
s
, maximal with respect to prefix order and containing
no transition involving
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}
.
System
\mathbf{S}
is
lock-free
if for each

s\in\mathsf{R}({(\hskip-2.2pt|\mathbf{S}|\hskip-2.2pt)})
,
s
is not a lock.

•

Deadlock freedom
: a configuration

s\in\mathsf{R}({(\hskip-2.2pt|\mathbf{S}|\hskip-2.2pt)})
is a
deadlock
if

s
has no outgoing transitions in
(\hskip-2.2pt|\mathbf{S}|\hskip-2.2pt)
, yet there exists

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}
such that
s(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}})
has an outgoing transition
in
M_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}
.
System
\mathbf{S}
is
deadlock-free
if for each

s\in\mathsf{R}({(\hskip-2.2pt|\mathbf{S}|\hskip-2.2pt)})
,
s
is not a
deadlock.

It is the case that lock-freedom, strong lock-freedom, and
deadlock-freedom of
\hat{\mathbf{S}}
(in the sense of

Sections

4
,
4
and
4
) respectively imply liveness,
lock-freedom, and deadlock-freedom of
\mathbf{S}
as stated in

Section

6
below, which relies on the following lemma.

Lemma
.

Let
\mathbf{S}=(M_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}})_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}}
be a system of
CFSMs.
If
w\in\mathbf{L}((\hskip-2.2pt|\mathbf{S}|\hskip-2.2pt))
is recognised by a run of

(\hskip-2.2pt|\mathbf{S}|\hskip-2.2pt)
from
s_{0}
to
s
then, for each

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}
,
w{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}}}\in\mathbf{L}(M_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}})
and

w{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}}}
is recognised by a run of
M_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}
from

s_{0}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}})
to
s(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}})
.

Proof.

By induction on the length of
w
using
Section

6
.
∎

Proposition
.

For all systems of CFSMs

\mathbf{S}=(M_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}})_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}}

(1)

\hat{\mathbf{S}}
lock-free iff
\mathbf{S}
live;

(2)

{\hat{\mathbf{S}}}
strong lock-free iff
\mathbf{S}
lock-free;

(3)

{\hat{\mathbf{S}}}
deadlock-free iff
\mathbf{S}

deadlock-free.

Proof.

1

\Rightarrow
Let
{\hat{\mathbf{S}}}
be
lock-free.
Following
Section

6
, in order to show the liveness of

\mathbf{S}
, let us consider
s\in\mathsf{R}({(\hskip-2.2pt|\mathbf{S}|\hskip-2.2pt)})
, and let

s(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}})\xrightarrow{\mathsf{a}}{}
be a transition in
M_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}
(for a
certain
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}
).
Let now
w
be the trace of a run of
(\hskip-2.2pt|\mathbf{S}|\hskip-2.2pt)

from
s_{0}
to
s
.
By
Section

6

w\in{\llbracket\hat{\mathbf{S}}\rrbracket}
and, by

Section

6
,
w{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}}}\in\mathbf{L}(M_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}})
and

w{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}}}
is the trace of a run from
s_{0}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}})
to

s(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}})
in
M_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}
.
Now, to prove that
{\mathbf{S}}
is live, we have to show that there
exists a run of
(\hskip-2.2pt|\mathbf{S}|\hskip-2.2pt)
from
s
such that one of its
transitions has a component transition from
s(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}})
.
We have that
w{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}}}\cat\mathsf{a}\in\mathbf{L}(M_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}})
.
Hence, by lock-freedom of
{\hat{\mathbf{S}}}
, there is
w^{\prime}
such
that
w\cat w^{\prime}\in{\llbracket\hat{\mathbf{S}}\rrbracket}
and

w^{\prime}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}}}\neq\varepsilon
.
By
Section

6

w^{\prime}
is also a run of
(\hskip-2.2pt|\mathbf{S}|\hskip-2.2pt)
from

s
and, by
Section

6
(considering the CFSMs

\mathbf{S}^{\prime}=(M^{\prime}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}})_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}}
where each

M^{\prime}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}
is like
M^{\prime}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}
but with

s(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}})
as initial state), such a run has a transition
from
s(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}})
as component transition.

\Leftarrow

By contraposition, let us assume
{\hat{\mathbf{S}}}
not to be lock-free.
Then there exists a participant
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}
and a word

w\in{\llbracket\hat{\mathbf{S}}\rrbracket}
such that

•

w{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}
is not maximal in
\hat{\mathbf{S}}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}})
; and

•

for all
w^{\prime}
,
w\cat w^{\prime}\in{\llbracket\hat{\mathbf{S}}\rrbracket}
implies
w^{\prime}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}=\varepsilon

Let
s
be the configuration in
(\hskip-2.2pt|\mathbf{S}|\hskip-2.2pt)
reached by
recognising
w
. Since
w{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}
is not maximal, we
get, by
Section

6
and determinism of the automata in

\mathbf{S}
, that
s(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}})
has at least an outgoing transition. If

\mathbf{S}
were live, then there would be a run of
(\hskip-2.2pt|\mathbf{S}|\hskip-2.2pt)
from

s
– and hence a word of the form

w\cat w^{\prime}\in\mathbf{L}{((\hskip-2.2pt|\mathbf{S}|\hskip-2.2pt))}={\llbracket\hat{\mathbf{S}}\rrbracket}
–
including a transition involving
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}
, so contradicting the liveness
of
\mathbf{S}
. So
\mathbf{S}
is not live and we are done.

The proof of
2
and
3
are
similar to
1
.
∎

Remark
.

Section

6
does not hold in case we consider
non-determistic CFSMs.
For instance, let us consider the following system of CFSMs.

\mathbf{S}=(M_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}})_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}\in\{\,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}},{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}\,\}}
where

q_{0}
M_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}
q_{1}
q_{2}

{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{!}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}}{}

{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{!}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{n}}}{}

q_{0}
M_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}
q_{1}
q_{2}
q_{3}
q_{4}
q_{5}
\varepsilon
\varepsilon

{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}}{}

{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}}{}

{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{n}}}{}

The corresponding communicating systems is

\hat{\mathbf{S}}=(\mathbb{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}})_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}\in\{\,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}},{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}\,\}}

where

\mathbb{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}=\{\,\varepsilon,\,\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{!}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}}{}$},\,\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{!}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}}{}$}\cat\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{!}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{n}}}{}$}\,\}\qquad\mathbb{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}=\{\,\varepsilon,\,\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}}{}$},\,\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}}{}$}\cat\scalebox{.8}{${\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{n}}}{}$}\,\}

It is easy to check that
\hat{\mathbf{S}}
is deadlock-free.
However
\mathbf{S}
is not so, since the system can reach the stuck
configuration
(q_{1},q_{5})
and there is an outgoing transition from

q_{1}
in
M_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}
.
Likewise, the communicating system made of
M_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}

and either of the following machines

q_{0}
q_{0}
q_{0}
q_{0}

{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}}{}

{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}}{}

{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{n}}}{}

q_{0}
q_{0}
q_{0}
q_{0}
q_{0}
\varepsilon

{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}}{}

{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}}{}

{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}{\,}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{n}}}{}

also reaches a deadlock configuration.

The FCL formalism, by abstracting from the notion of state, is in fact
intrinsically deterministic.

7.
Choreography Automata and Choreography Languages

We advocated
choreography automata

(c-automata)
[
BLT20
]
as an expressive and flexible model of
global specifications.
Essentially c-automata are finite-state automata (FSAs) whose
transitions are labelled with interactions.
This yields an immediate connection between g-languages and
c-automata in terms of the languages the latter accept.
This section explores such connection, as well as the connection
between the projection operation on c-automata in
[
BLT20
]
and the
projection on g-languages.

7.1.
The g-languages of c-automata

Choreography automata
(c-automata for short, ranged over by

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A}
,
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{B}
, etc.) are defined in
[
BLT20
]
as
deterministic FSAs with labels in the set
\Sigma_{\text{int}}
of interactions.
(Observe that the set of participants occurring in a c-automaton is
necessarily finite.)
We can see c-automata as a tool for specifying g-languages
as shown by the next proposition.

Proposition
.

Given a c-automaton
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A}
,
\mathbf{L}(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A})
is a continuous
g-language.

Proof.

To show that
\mathbf{L}(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A})
is a g-language we need to show prefix
closure. It follows from the fact that all the states are accepting:
if a word
w
is in
\mathbf{L}(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A})
then any prefix of
w
can be
generated by taking the corresponding prefix of the computation
generating
w
.

We now need to show that the language is continuous.
We need to show that
\mathbf{L}(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A})
contains an
infinite word if it contains all its finite prefixes. Note that,
thanks to determinism, words which are prefixes one of the other are
generated by computations which are prefixes one of the other as
well. Let
w
be an infinite word whose prefixes are in

\mathbf{L}(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A})
. The infinite run obtained as the limit of the runs
generating the prefixes of
w
generates
w
. This concludes the
proof.
∎

Interestingly, c-automata have an immediate relation with
CFSMs
[
BZ83
]
, introduced in the previous section, which can be
adopted to model the local behaviour of distributed components.
Indeed, the local behaviour of a participant of a c-automaton
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A}

can be algorithmically obtained directly from
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A}
.
Formally
3
3

3

Overloading the projection operator of
Section

2
does
not introduce confusion and avoids the introduction of further
notation.

Definition

(Projection of c-automata
[
BLT20
]
)
.

Let
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A}=\langle{\mathcal{S},q_{0},\Sigma_{\text{int}},\to}\rangle
be a c-automaton
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}
be a participant.
The
projection of
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A}
on
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}
is the CFSM

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}}}
obtained by determinising up-to-language
equivalence the
intermediate
automaton

\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}=\langle{\mathcal{S},q_{0},\Sigma_{\text{act}}\cup\{\,\varepsilon\,\},\{\,q\xrightarrow{\lambda{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}}}}q^{\prime}\;\big|\;q\xrightarrow{\lambda}q^{\prime}\,\}}\rangle

The
projection of
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A}
, written
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}
, is the
system of CFSMs
(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}}})_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}}
.

The l-language of a projection of a c-automaton
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A}
coincides
with the projection of the language of
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A}
:

Proposition
.

If
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A}
is a c-automaton on
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}
then for all

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}
,

\mathbf{L}(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A}){\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}=\mathbf{L}(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}})
.

Proof.

By definition of projection and since determinisation preserves the
language, we have

\mathbf{L}(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}})=\mathbf{L}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}})
, where

\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}
is the intermediate automaton
(cf.
Section

7.1
).
Since
\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}
is obtained by taking the projection of
every transition in
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A}
,

\mathbf{L}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}})=\mathbf{L}(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A}){\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}
.
The thesis follows by transitivity.
∎

Observing that
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}
is
\varepsilon
-free, the LTS of

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}
is a c-automaton and its language coincides with the
g-language of the system

(\mathbf{L}(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}))_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}}
thanks to

Section

6
.

The notion of well-formedness we provided in
[
BLT20
]
was aimed to
guarantee correctness and completeness as well as the communication
properties of projected systems (cf.
Section

6
).
We discovered later on that our notion of well-formedness
in
[
BLT20
]
was flawed.
In fact it does not correctly handle the interplay between concurrent
transitions and choices.
This is shown by the following example.

Example
.

The c-automaton
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A}
below

q_{0}
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A}
q_{1}
q_{2}
q_{3}
q_{4}
q_{5}
q_{6}

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{D}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{n}}}

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{r}}}

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{D}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{n}}}

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{r}}}

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{r}}}

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{D}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{n}}}

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}

is well-formed according to
[
BLT20
, Def. 4.12]
.
However, the system
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}
admits the run

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{r}}}

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{D}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{n}}}

which is not a word accepted by

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A}
.

\diamond

In our setting, the problem of the above
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A}
is that

\mathbf{L}(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A})
is not CUI.
In fact, by setting
w_{1}=\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}}$}
and

w_{2}=\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{D}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{n}}}$}
, we have that

w_{1}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{r}}}$},w_{2}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{r}}}$}\in\mathbf{L}(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A})
.
Now, by taking
w=\varepsilon
, we have that

w_{1}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}}}=w{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}}}
and

w_{2}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}=w{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}
, but

w\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{r}}}$}=\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{r}}}$}\not\in\mathbf{L}(\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A})
.

7.2.
Deciding CUI and Branch-Awareness

In this section we show that CUI and BA are decidable when
restricting to c-languages associated to c-automata.
The approach of the present paper can hence be used to prove the
communication properties considered in
[
BLT20
]
(namely those in

Section

6
) by showing the corresponding ones in the FCL
setting.

We begin by unveiling an interesting interplay between c-automata
and FCLs.
Languages accepted by c-automata are continuous closures of regular
languages, so making conditions like CUI and branch-awareness
decidable.

Theorem
.

CUI is decidable on languages accepted by c-automata.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
be a language accepted by a c-automaton
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A}
.
We show that
\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{cui}}({\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}})
is decidable by reducing the problem to a
search in the FSAs
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A}
,
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}
, and
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}
.

By definition of CUI we have
\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{cui}}({\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}})
iff there are an
interaction
\alpha=\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}$}
and finite words

w_{1},w_{2},w\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
such that

w\cat\alpha\not\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}\qquad w{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}=w_{1}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}\qquad w{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}=w_{1}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}\qquad\text{while}\qquad w_{1}\cat\alpha,w_{2}\cat\alpha\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}

This amounts to find a state
q
of
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A}
and two states
4
4

4

Recall that by definition of projection the states of

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}
and
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}
are subsets of the states of

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A}
due to the determinisation of the intermediate automata.

Q_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}
and
Q_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}
respectively in

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}
and
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}
such that

q\not\xrightarrow{\alpha}\quad q\in Q_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}\cap Q_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}\quad\text{while there are}\quad q_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}\in Q_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}},q_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}\in Q_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}\text{ s.t. }q_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}\xrightarrow{\alpha}\text{ and }q_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}\xrightarrow{\alpha}

Since all the involved FSAs are finite an exhaustive search can determine
if such states exist for each interaction.
∎

Theorem
.

BA is decidable on languages accepted by c-automata.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
be a language accepted by a c-automaton
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A}
and
let
\mathbf{A}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}
be the intermediate automaton obtained as in

Section

7.1
on a participant
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}
of
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A}
.
Given a state
p
of an FSA
A
, we let
[A]_{p}
denote the FSA
obtained by replacing the initial state of
A
with
p
and let

[A]^{p}
denote the FSA obtained by making
p
the only final state
in
A
(i.e., setting the final states of
A
to the singleton

\{\,p\,\}
).

In order to get a decision procedure for
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{ba}}({\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}})
, we observe that the
following equivalences do hold.

\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{ba}}({\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}})

iff there are
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}
and two maximal words

w_{1}\neq w_{2}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}
such that

w_{1}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}\prec w_{2}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}

iff there are
{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{P}}
, two states
p\neq q
of

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A}
such that

(1)

\mathbf{L}([\mathbf{A}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}]^{p})\cap\mathbf{L}([\mathbf{A}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}]^{q})\neq\emptyset
;

(2)

there are two maximal words
w_{1}^{\prime}\in\mathbf{L}([\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A}]_{p})

and
w_{2}^{\prime}\in\mathbf{L}([\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A}]_{q})
such that

w_{1}^{\prime}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}=\varepsilon
and

w_{2}^{\prime}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}\neq\varepsilon
.

Intuitively, the first equivalence above states
that for a participant
{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}
it is not possible to distinguish when
a computation halts with word
w_{1}
or continues as

w_{2}
.
Transferring this condition on automata yields the last equivalence,
which basically requires that

\mathbf{L}([\mathbf{A}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}]^{p})\cap\mathbf{L}([\mathbf{A}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}]^{q})\neq\emptyset
.
Then from its initial state
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A}
has two paths from the initial
state respectively reaching
p
and
q
with two words whose projection
on
{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}
coincide.
Therefore there are two runs from
p
and
q
respectively such that

{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}
does not occur in the run from
p
while it occurs in the run
from
q
, as required by
Item

2
.

We now notice that
Item

1
is decidable because the
intersection of
\omega
-regular languages is computatable.
In order to decide
Item

2
, instead, we can proceed as follows.
We perform a breath-first search on
[\mathbf{A}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}]^{p}
stopping
either when a state does not have outgoing transitions or when it
has been already visited.

Item

2
holds iff the resulting tree has a path from

p
to a leaf made of
\varepsilon
-transitions only.
Finiteness of automata guarantees that this procedure terminates.
Likewise we can check the existence of a word

w^{\prime}_{2}\in\mathbf{L}([\mathbb{C}\mathrm{A}]_{q})
such that

w^{\prime}_{2}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}\neq\varepsilon
(as required by
Item

2
).
∎

8.
Global Types as Choreographic Languages

The global types of
[
SD19
]
are our last case study.
Informally, a global type
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\to{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}:\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}_{i}.{\sf G}_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
specifies a
protocol where participant
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}
must send to
{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}
a message
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}_{i}

for some
1\leq i\leq n
and then, depending on which
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}_{i}
was
chosen by
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}
, the protocol continues as
{\sf G}_{i}
.
Global types and multiparty sessions are defined in
[
SD19
]

in terms of the following grammars for, respectively,
pre-global
types
,
pre-processes
, and
pre-multiparty sessions
(we
adapted some of the notation to our setting):

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\begin{array}[]{lcl}{\sf G}&::=^{\textrm{co}}&\mathtt{end}\\
&\;\ \big|&\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\to{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}:\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}_{i}.{\sf G}_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}\\
\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathit{P}&::=^{\textrm{co}}&\mathbf{0}\\
&\;\ \big|&\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}?\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}_{i}.\mathit{P}_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}\\[2.84526pt]
&\;\ \big|&\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}!\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}_{i}.\mathit{P}_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{M}&::=&\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\,\triangleright\mathit{P}\\
&\;\ \big|&\mathcal{M}\!\!\;\ \big|\ \;\!\!\mathcal{M}\\
\end{array}\end{array}

where we assume messages
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}_{i}
to be pairwise distinct in sets

\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}_{i}.{\sf G}_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
and
\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}_{i}.\mathit{P}_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
, to
whose elements we call
branches
.
The first two grammars are interpreted coinductively, that is their
solutions are both minimal and maximal fixpoints (the latter
corresponding to infinite trees).
A pre-global type
{\sf G}
(resp. pre-process
\mathit{P}
)
is a
global type
(resp.
process
) if its tree
representation is
regular
, namely it has finitely many distinct
sub-trees.
A
multiparty session
(MPS for short) is a pre-multiparty
session such that (a) in
{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}\,\triangleright{\mathit{P}}
, participant
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}
does not occur in process
\mathit{P}
and (b) in

{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}_{1}\,\triangleright}{\mathit{P}_{1}}\mid\ldots\mid{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}_{n}}\,\triangleright{\mathit{P}_{n}}
, participants
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}_{i}
are pairwise
different.

The semantics of global types is the LTS induced by
the following two rules:

\begin{array}[]{c}\\
{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 60.43756pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=120.87512pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\text{(GT1)}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\to{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}:\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}_{i}.{\sf G}_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\rightarrow{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}:\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}_{i}}{\sf G}_{i}$}}}}\\[20.00003pt]
{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\text{for each }1\leq i\leq n\quad{{\sf G}_{i}}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\rightarrow{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}:\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}}{\sf G}^{\prime}_{i}\qquad\{\,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}},{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}\,\}\cap\{\,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{R}},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{S}}\,\}=\emptyset$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=236.49701pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\text{(GT2)}$}}}\hbox{\kern 25.75356pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{R}}\to\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{S}}:\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}_{i}.{\sf G}_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\rightarrow{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}:\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{R}}\to\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{S}}:\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}_{i}.{\sf G}^{\prime}_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}$}}}}\\[20.00003pt]
\end{array}

Rule
(GT2)
allows out-of-order execution, namely interaction

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}

between participants
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}
can happen even if an
interaction between other participants is syntactically occurring
before, provided that

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}

occurs in all branches.
As usual, we let

{\sf G}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{0}\cat\cdots\cat\alpha_{n}}{\sf G}^{\prime}
to denote

{\sf G}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{0}}{\sf G}_{1}\cdots{\sf G}_{n}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{n}}{\sf G}^{\prime}
and always assume that

{\sf G}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}{\sf G}
holds.

The semantics for MPSs is the LTS defined by the following rule

\displaystyle\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\triangleright{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}!(\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}.\mathit{P}\}\uplus\Lambda)\;\ \big|\ \;{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}\triangleright\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}?(\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}.\mathit{P}^{\prime}\}\uplus\Lambda^{\prime})\;\ \big|\ \;\mathcal{M}\quad\xrightarrow{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\rightarrow{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}:\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}}\quad\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\triangleright\mathit{P}\;\ \big|\ \;{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}\triangleright\mathit{P}^{\prime}\;\ \big|\ \;\mathcal{M}

(2)

where

\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}_{i}:\mathit{P}_{i}\}_{i\in I}\uplus\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}_{j}^{\prime}:\mathit{P}^{\prime}_{j}\}_{j\in J}=\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}_{i}:\mathit{P}_{i}\}_{i\in I}\cup\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}_{j}^{\prime}:\mathit{P}^{\prime}_{j}\}_{j\in J}
if

\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}_{i}\}_{i\in I}\cap\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}^{\prime}_{j}\}_{j\in J}=\emptyset
,
otherwise

\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}_{i}:\mathit{P}_{i}\}_{i\in I}\uplus\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}_{j}^{\prime}:\mathit{P}^{\prime}_{j}\}_{j\in J}
is undefined.

Rule (
2
) applies only if the messages in
\Lambda^{\prime}

include those in
\Lambda
, which is the case for MPSs obtained by
projection, defined below.

Definition

(Projection
[
SD19
, Definition 3.4]
)
.

The
projection
of
{\sf G}
on a participant
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}
such that the
depths of its occurrences in
{\sf G}
are bounded is the partial
function
{\sf G}\!\upharpoonright_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}
coinductively defined by

\mathtt{end}\!\upharpoonright_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}=\mathbf{0}
and, for a global type

{\sf G}=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\to{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}:\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}_{i}.{\sf G}_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
, by:

{\sf G}\!\upharpoonright_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{0}&\text{if $\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}$ is
not a participant of }{\sf G}\\
{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}!\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}_{i}.{\sf G}_{i}\!\upharpoonright_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}&\text{if }\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\\
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}?\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}_{i}.{\sf G}_{i}\!\upharpoonright_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}&\text{if }\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}={\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}\\
{\sf G}_{1}\!\upharpoonright_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}&\text{if }\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}\not\in\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}},{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}\}\text{ and }n=1\\
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{S}}?(\Lambda_{1}\uplus\ldots\uplus\Lambda_{n})&\text{if }\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}\not\in\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}},{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}\},\ n>1,\text{ and }\text{for all }1\leq i\leq n,{\sf G}_{i}\!\upharpoonright_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{S}}?\Lambda_{i}\end{cases}

The global type
{\sf G}
is
projectable
5
5

5

In
[
SD19
]
, projectability embeds well-branchedness.

if
{\sf G}\!\upharpoonright_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}
is defined for all participants
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}
of

{\sf G}
, in which case
{\sf G}\!\upharpoonright
denotes the corresponding MPS.

The projection on
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}
is partial because if we have a choice where

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}
is not involved in the first interaction then only the last
clause in
Section

8
could apply, but this clause
requires
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}
to start each branch with an input action from the
same sender
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{S}}
.

Let
\widehat{\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}}({\sf G})
be the language coinductively defined as follows:

\widehat{\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}}(\mathtt{end})=\{\,\varepsilon\,\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\widehat{\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\to{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}:\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}_{i}.{\sf G}_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n})=\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq n}\{\,\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}_{\mathit{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}}$}\cat w\;\big|\;w\in\widehat{\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}}({\sf G}_{i})\,\}

The g-language
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}({\sf G})
associated to a global type
{\sf G}
is the concurrency and
prefix closure of
\widehat{\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}}({\sf G})
, that is

\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}({\sf G})={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{pref}}({\{\,w\in\Sigma_{\text{int}}^{\infty}\;\big|\;\text{ there is }w^{\prime}\in\widehat{\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}}({\sf G})\text{ such that }w\sim w^{\prime}\,\}})

We define the l-language
\mathbb{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}({\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}\triangleright\mathit{P})
associated
to a named process
{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}\triangleright\mathit{P}
as the prefix closure of

\widehat{\mathbb{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}}({\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}\triangleright\mathit{P})
which, letting

\star\in\{\,?,!\,\}
, is defined by

\begin{array}[]{l}\widehat{\mathbb{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}}({\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}\triangleright\mathbf{0})=\{\,\varepsilon\,\}\quad\text{and}\quad\widehat{\mathbb{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}}({\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}\triangleright\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\!\star\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}_{i}.\mathit{P}_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n})=\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq n}\{\,\text{\small$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}\!\star\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}_{\mathit{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}$}\cat w\;\big|\;w\in\widehat{\mathbb{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}}(\mathit{P}_{i})\,\}\end{array}

The system associated to an MPS is defined as the following map:

S({\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}_{1}\,\triangleright}{\mathit{P}_{1}}\mid\ldots\mid{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}_{n}}\,\triangleright{\mathit{P}_{n}})=\{\,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}_{i}\mapsto\mathbb{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}_{i}\,\triangleright\mathit{P}_{i})\;\big|\;1\leq i\leq n\,\}

Our constructions capture relevant properties of the
global types in
[
SD19
]
.

We establish correspondences between the two frameworks in

Sections

8
and
8
.

Proposition
.

Given a projectable global type
{\sf G}
,

\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}({\sf G})=\{\,w\;\big|\;{\sf G}\xrightarrow{w}\,\}
.

Proof.

We show the two inclusions.

\subseteq

We begin by showing

\widehat{\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}}({\sf G})\subseteq\{\,w\;\big|\;{\sf G}\xrightarrow{w}\,\}
.
If
{\sf G}=\mathtt{end}
then the thesis trivially follows since

{\sf G}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}
.
If
{\sf G}=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\to{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}:\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}_{i}.{\sf G}_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
then each word in

\widehat{\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}}({\sf G})
begins with an interaction

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}_{\mathit{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}}

with
1\leq i\leq n
.
By the reduction semantics of global types,

{\sf G}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}_{\mathit{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}}$}}{\sf G}_{i}
.
By coinduction
[
KS17
]
we obtain the thesis.
Each word in
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}({\sf G})
is a prefix of a concurrency equivalent
word
w\in\widehat{\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}}({\sf G})
.
Hence we obtain the thesis by observing that

{\sf G}\xrightarrow{w}
from the previous argument and by
(possibly repeated) application of rule (GT2) of the semantics of
global types.

\supseteq

If
{\sf G}\xrightarrow{w}
then, for some
{\sf G}^{\prime}
we have

{\sf G}\xrightarrow{\alpha}{\sf G}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{w^{\prime}}
where

w=\alpha\cat w^{\prime}
.
If
{\sf G}\xrightarrow{\alpha}{\sf G}^{\prime}
is an instance of the first rule of
the operational semantics of global types then the thesis follows by
coinduction.
Otherwise, assuming
{\sf G}=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{R}}\to\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{S}}:\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}_{i}.{\sf G}_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
, for
each
1\leq i\leq n
we have (i)
{\sf G}_{i}\xrightarrow{\alpha}{\sf G}^{\prime}_{i}
,
(ii)
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{ptp}}({\alpha})\cap\{\,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{R}},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{S}}\,\}=\emptyset
, and (iii)

{\sf G}^{\prime}=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{R}}\to\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{S}}:\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}_{i}.{\sf G}^{\prime}_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}\xrightarrow{w^{\prime}}
.
By coinduction,
w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}({\sf G}^{\prime})
hence

w=\alpha\cat w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}({\sf G})
.
∎

We now relate projectability (cf.
Section

8
) and
our properties.

Proposition
.

If
{\sf G}
is a projectable global type then
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}({\sf G})
is a CUI and
branch-aware sc-language.

Proof.

Let
{\sf G}=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\to{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}:\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}_{i}.{\sf G}_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
be a global type projectable on

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}
.
By construction,
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}({\sf G})\subseteq\Sigma_{\text{int}}^{\star}
or
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}({\sf G})

is continuous.

To show that
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}({\sf G})
is branch-aware we proceed by
contradiction: assume that
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}({\sf G})
is not branch-aware.
Then there exist two distinct maximal words in
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}({\sf G})
, say

w=\alpha_{0}\cat\alpha_{1}\cdots
and

w^{\prime}=\alpha^{\prime}_{0}\cat\alpha^{\prime}_{1}\cdots
, such that

w\not\sim w^{\prime}
and, for a participant
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}
, we have

w{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}\prec w^{\prime}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}
, that is

w{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}
is a proper prefix of
w^{\prime}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}
.
Note that
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}
must occur in
{\sf G}
otherwise,

{\sf G}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}=\mathbf{0}
.
By definition of
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}({\sf G})
, there are

\widehat{w}=\alpha_{0}\cat\alpha_{1}\cdots
and

\widehat{w^{\prime}}=\alpha^{\prime}_{0}\cat\alpha^{\prime}_{1}\cdots
in
\widehat{\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}}({\sf G})
,
such that
w\sim\widehat{w}
and

w^{\prime}\sim\widehat{w^{\prime}}
; we have

\widehat{w}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}\prec\widehat{w}^{\prime}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}
.

By
Section

8
, there are two runs

{\sf G}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{0}}{\sf G}_{1}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{2}}\cdots\qquad\text{and}\qquad{\sf G}\xrightarrow{\alpha^{\prime}_{0}}{\sf G}^{\prime}_{1}\xrightarrow{\alpha^{\prime}_{2}}\cdots

Let
h
be the index such that
\alpha_{h}\neq\alpha^{\prime}_{h}
and

\alpha_{i}=\alpha^{\prime}_{i}
for all
0\leq i<h
; note that
h
must
exist otherwise
\widehat{w}=\widehat{w}^{\prime}
,
contradicting
w\not\sim w^{\prime}
.
Necessarily,
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}
does not occur in
\alpha_{h}
otherwise

(\alpha_{0}\cat\cdots\cat\alpha_{h}){\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}\not\prec(\alpha^{\prime}_{0}\cat\cdots\cat\alpha^{\prime}_{h}){\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}
contradicting

\widehat{w}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}\prec\widehat{w}^{\prime}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}
.

Take the last index, say
n
, of the interaction in

\widehat{w}
involving
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}
, which necessarily exists
otherwise
\widehat{w}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}
cannot be a proper prefix of

\widehat{w}^{\prime}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}}}
.
This contradicts the projectability of
{\sf G}
since its subtree
{\sf G}_{h}

has two branches initiating with
\alpha_{h}
and
\alpha^{\prime}_{h}
on which
the projections for
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}}
differ.
Hence, none of the cases of
Section

8
can be
applied.

To show CUI we have to prove that if there are words

w_{1}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}$},w_{2}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}$},w\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}({\sf G})

such that
{w_{1}}\downarrow_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}={w}\downarrow_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}
and

{w_{2}}\downarrow_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}={w}\downarrow_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}}
then

w\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}$}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}({\sf G})
.

By definition of
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}({\sf G})
, from

w_{1}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}$},w_{2}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}$},w\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}({\sf G})

we can infer that there exist

\hat{w}_{1},\hat{w}_{2},\hat{w}\in\widehat{\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}}({\sf G})
and

w^{\prime}_{1},w^{\prime}_{2},w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}({\sf G})
such that

•

\hat{w}_{1}\sim w^{\prime}_{1}

\hat{w}_{2}\sim w^{\prime}_{2}

\hat{w}\sim w^{\prime}
;

•

w^{\prime}_{i}=w_{i}\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}$}\cat w^{\prime\prime}_{i}
with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{ptp}}({w^{\prime\prime}_{i}})\cap\{\,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}},{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}\,\}=\emptyset
(
i\in\{\,1,2\,\}
);

•

w^{\prime}=w\cat w^{\prime\prime}
with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{ptp}}({w^{\prime\prime}})\cap\{\,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}},{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}\,\}=\emptyset
.

Let us now consider the following cases.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{ptp}}({w^{\prime}})\cap\{\,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}},{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}\,\}=\emptyset

From the hypothesis
w^{\prime}_{1}
contains exactly one interaction
involving
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}
and such an interaction is

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}

.
We proceed now by coinduction on the projection of

{\sf G}=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{R}}\to\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{S}}:\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}_{i}.{\sf G}_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
on
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}

(cf.
Section

8
).
Since the first action of
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}
is an output we necessarily have
a sequence of co-inductive cases where we apply the 4-th case
of
Section

8
followed by a case where
the 2-nd case of
Section

8
is applied.
Hence,
{\sf G}
has no branch before the interaction

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}

which
must occur in all the maximal words, as desired.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{ptp}}({w^{\prime}})\cap\{\,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}},{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}\,\}\neq\emptyset

Let

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{D}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{x}}}

be the first interaction in
w^{\prime}

(hence also in
\hat{w}
)
such that
\{\,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{D}}\,\}\cap\{\,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}},{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}\,\}\neq\emptyset
.
With no loss of generality, assume
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{C}}
; the other cases
are analogous and therefore omitted.

By
{w_{1}}\downarrow_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}={w}\downarrow_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}
, the interaction

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{D}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{x}}}

is present also in
w^{\prime}_{1}
and it is the
first one having
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}
as participant. Moreover, the same holds
for
\hat{w}_{1}
. Let us proceed by coinduction on the
projection of

{\sf G}=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{S}}\rightarrow\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{R}}:\{\,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}_{i}.{\sf G}_{i}\,\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
on
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}
. The case
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{R}}=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}
follows by coinduction since
both
\hat{w}
and
\hat{w}_{1}
are generated from the
same branch since
w{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}=w_{1}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}
. The
case
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\not\in\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{R}},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{S}}\}
and
n=1
follows by coinduction.

Let us consider the case

\{\,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{S}},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{R}}\,\}\cap\{\,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}\,\}=\emptyset
, and
assume that
\hat{w}_{1}
and
\hat{w}
begin, respectively with

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{S}}\rightarrow\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{R}}:\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}_{u}
and

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{S}}\rightarrow\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{R}}:\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}_{v}
with
u\neq v
(the case
u=v
is simpler).
Let now
{\sf G}_{u}\!\upharpoonright_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{D}}?{\Lambda_{u}}
,
and
{\sf G}_{v}\!\upharpoonright_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{D}}?{\Lambda_{v}}
(which are defined by projectability
of
{\sf G}
).
By what said previously, we have that

{\Lambda_{u}}=\{\,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{x}}.P^{\prime}\uplus\ldots\,\}
and
{\Lambda_{u}}=\{\,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{x}}.P^{\prime\prime}\uplus\ldots\,\}
.
Now, since

{\sf G}\!\upharpoonright_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{D}}?\{\,{\Lambda_{u}}\uplus{\Lambda_{v}}\uplus\ldots\,\}
,
we can infer that
P^{\prime}=P^{\prime\prime}
.
This implies that
w\cat\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}$}\in\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}({\sf G})
.
This concludes the proof.
∎

The proof of
Section

8
below uses the following auxiliary lemma.

Lemma
.

If
{\sf G}\!\upharpoonright_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}
is defined then
w\in\widehat{\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}}({\sf G})
implies

w{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}}}}\in\widehat{\mathbb{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}}({\sf G}\!\upharpoonright_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}})
.

Proof.

By coinduction on
{\sf G}
.
If
{\sf G}=\mathtt{end}
the thesis trivially hold.
If
{\sf G}=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\to{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}:\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}_{i}.{\sf G}_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
or

{\sf G}={\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}}\to\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}:\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}_{i}.{\sf G}_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
then the thesis immediately
follows by coinduction observing that

w=\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}_{\mathit{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}}$}\cat w^{\prime}
or

w=\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{B}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}_{\mathit{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}}$}\cat w^{\prime}
for some
1\leq i\leq n
and

w^{\prime}\in\widehat{\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}}({\sf G}_{i})
.
If
{\sf G}=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{R}}\to\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{S}}:\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}_{i}.{\sf G}_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
with
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\neq\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{R}}
and

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\neq\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{S}}
then

w=\scalebox{.8}{$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{R}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\xrightarrow{\scriptstyle{}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{S}}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\colon}\!\!{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}_{\mathit{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i}}}}}$}\cat w^{\prime}
for some
1\leq i\leq n
and

w^{\prime}\in\widehat{\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}}({\sf G}_{i})
; hence

w\!\upharpoonright_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}=w^{\prime}\!\upharpoonright_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}
.
By
Section

8
, either (a)
n=1
and

{\sf G}\!\upharpoonright_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}={\sf G}_{1}\!\upharpoonright_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}
or (b)
n>1
and

{\sf G}\!\upharpoonright_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{S}}?(\Lambda_{1}\uplus\ldots\uplus\Lambda_{n})
where, for all
1\leq i\leq n
,

{\sf G}_{i}\!\upharpoonright_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{S}}?\Lambda_{i}
and
\Lambda_{i}
of the
form
\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}^{i}_{1}:{\sf G}^{i}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}^{i}_{n_{i}}:{\sf G}^{i}_{n_{i}}\}
.
In case (a) the thesis immediately follows by coinduction observing
that
i=n=1
.
In case (b),

w^{\prime}\!\upharpoonright_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}}=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{S}}?\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{m}}_{ij}\cat v
with

1\leq j\leq n_{i}
and, coinductively,

v\in\widehat{\mathbb{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}\triangleright{\sf G}_{i}\!\upharpoonright_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}})
the
projection on
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}
of some word in
\widehat{\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}}({\sf G}_{i})
otherwise we
would violate the projectability of
{\sf G}
on
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}
.
∎

Proposition
.

Given a projectable global type
{\sf G}
,

{\llbracket S({\sf G}\!\upharpoonright)\rrbracket}=\{\,w\;\big|\;{\sf G}\!\upharpoonright\xrightarrow{w}\,\}
.

Proof.

We get

{\llbracket S({\sf G}\!\upharpoonright)\rrbracket}=\{\,w\;\big|\;{\sf G}\!\upharpoonright\xrightarrow{w}\,\}
by showing that

\displaystyle S({\sf G}\!\upharpoonright)=

\displaystyle\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}({\sf G}){\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}

(3)

Indeed,
{\llbracket S({\sf G}\!\upharpoonright)\rrbracket}={\llbracket\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}({\sf G}){\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}\rrbracket}
by

Section

8
.

Section

8
ensures
S({\sf G}\!\upharpoonright)
is correct and
complete, hence
{\llbracket S({\sf G}\!\upharpoonright)\rrbracket}=\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}({\sf G})
by

Sections

3
and
3
.
So, assume

{\sf G}\!\upharpoonright={\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}_{1}\,\triangleright}{\mathit{P}_{1}}\mid\ldots\mid{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}_{n}}\,\triangleright{\mathit{P}_{n}}
, then

\displaystyle\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}({\sf G}){\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}=

\displaystyle\{\,w\;\big|\;{\sf G}\xrightarrow{w}\,\}{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}}}}

by
Section

8

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}_{i}\mapsto\{\,w{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}_{I}}}}}\;\big|\;{\sf G}\xrightarrow{w}\,\}\ \;\big|\;\ 1\leq i\leq n\,\}

by
Section

2

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}_{i}\mapsto\{\,w{\downarrow}_{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}_{I}}}}}\;\big|\;w\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textsf{pref}}({\{\,w^{\prime}\in\widehat{\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}}({\sf G})\;\big|\;w\sim w^{\prime}\,\}})\,\}\!\;\big|\;\!1\leq i\leq n\,\}

by def. of
\mathcal{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}({\sf G})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}_{i}\mapsto\mathbb{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}L}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{A}}_{i}\,\triangleright\mathit{P}_{i})\;\big|\;1\leq i\leq n\,\}

by
